Sep 12, 2:28 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
Anders' proposal is in and objections are running. It's a genuinely good piece of design work, with the trade-offs named rather than hidden. The strongest move in it: **the trigram index is an accelerator, never a ranker.** `pg_trgm` accelerates the same `LIKE '%term%'` predicate rather than introducing `similarity()` scoring or rank-ordering. That's what keeps the PostgreSQL/SQLite divergence a pure performance gap for ASCII input — identical row sets, identical order, different speed — instead of the correctness gap Yusuf found. He also closed the gap directly by folding case explicitly on both sides (`LOWER(col) LIKE LOWER(pattern)`) rather than letting the defaults diverge, and named the residual honestly: PostgreSQL's `lower()` is locale-aware, SQLite's is ASCII-only, so non-ASCII folding still differs. A narrow, named residual is a different thing from a silent one. Three other calls I expect to survive: - `nil` means absent; a non-nil empty string is a **caller bug**, not a wildcard. Silently coercing `""` to "match everything" is how a full-table scan reaches production. - An empty query is legal and returns everything paginated — because whether a caller *may* list all profiles is 02's authorization policy, and the DAO is the wrong layer to invent one. The pagination ceiling is protection, not a policy veto. That's a correct read of the ownership boundary. - Offset pagination chosen over keyset, with the cost stated rather than glossed: offset pages are unstable under concurrent inserts, so a row can shift or repeat across pages. Accepted for a POC, named so 03 doesn't discover it. He also declined one thing from my own plan — the `ProfileSearchResult` struct — on the grounds that the triple is already the smallest surface and a struct buys extensibility nobody has use for. He's right, and declining a lead's proposed shape with a reason is the seat working. One area I'll be watching in the ruling: the case-folding decision and the index declaration have to agree with each other, and a predicate that transforms the indexed column has implications for whether the index is usable at all. I'm leaving that to Yusuf's pass rather than feeding him the objection — if he doesn't reach it, I'll add it myself at synthesis, which is a lead's call to make rather than something to route around the pattern. Still outstanding: Yusuf's objections, Anders' answers, then my S2 ruling; and Beatriz's Table B.

Another Claude session sent a message: <teammate-message teammate_id="yusuf-karadag" color="green"> {"type":"idle_notification","from":"yusuf-karadag","timestamp":"2026-09-12T21:28:26.052Z","idleReason":"available","result":"## S2 — Numbered objections to Anders' `ProfileQuery`\n\n**1. `ORDER BY name ASC` is not the same order on the two backends, and that changes which rows are on page 1.**\nPostgres sorts by the database's collation; SQLite's default is `BINARY`, byte order, in which every uppercase letter sorts before every lowercase one. `Zebra` precedes `apple` on SQLite and follows it on Postgres. The `id` tiebreak makes the order *total*, not *identical*. Under offset pagination a caller asking for page 1 gets a different row set per backend. This is the `LIKE` finding from S3 in a second location.\n*What would change my mind:* the contract names one collation explicitly in both DDL and `ORDER BY` — `COLLATE \"C\"` on Postgres, `BINARY` on SQLite — and the conformance suite asserts identical ordering.\n\n**2. `LOWER(name) LIKE …` will not use the trigram index as the index is currently specified.**\nS1's index list says \"trigram GIN on `user_profile.name`.\" A GIN index on the bare column is not used for a predicate on `lower(name)`. As written, the accelerator accelerates nothing and both backends do a full scan — the parity gap closes by making Postgres as slow as SQLite.\n*What would change my mind:* the index is respecified as an expression index on `lower(name)` with `gin_trgm_ops`, and S1's index list is corrected to match.\n\n**3. Caller input goes into a `LIKE` pattern unescaped.**\n`%` and `_` in `Name` are wildcards. A search for `50%` matches every row. Worse, the escape character itself differs: Postgres applies a default backslash escape, SQLite has none unless `ESCAPE` is given.\n*What would change my mind:* the contract states the DAO escapes `%`, `_` and the escape character, and emits an explicit `ESCAPE '\\'` on both backends.\n\n**4. Rejecting empty-after-trim removes the only way to find rows with a missing `region`.**\n`Region` is one of the address columns that will frequently be NULL on `source = 'idp_cache'` rows — the normal case, not the edge case. `nil` means \"don't filter\" and `\"\"` is a caller bug, so no legal query reaches those rows. That may be acceptable; it is currently unwritten.\n*What would change my mind:* nothing, if the contract says in one line that searching for absent address values is out of scope and callers must list-and-filter. I object to the silence, not the choice.\n\n**5. `total` can report a number the caller is forbidden to reach.**\nOffset ceilings at 10 000; `total` is exact and unbounded. The API can honestly return `total: 250000` and then reject page 201. Two documented behaviours that contradict each other at the boundary.\n*What would change my mind:* the contract states that `Offset > 10_000` returns `ErrInvalidQuery`, and that `total` is a magnitude, not a promise of reachability.\n\n**6. Pagination fields don't follow the discipline the filters do.**\nFilters use pointers to distinguish absent from empty; `Limit int` collapses \"unspecified\" and \"explicitly zero rows\" into `0`. Negative values are unaddressed.\n*What would change my mind:* a stated validation rule — `Limit < 0` or `> 100` and `Offset < 0` return `ErrInvalidQuery`; `0` means default, deliberately.\n\n**7. `ErrInvalidQuery` does not exist in S1's sentinel set.**\nThe proposal returns it in at least three places. S1 lists `ErrNotFound`, `ErrDuplicateUsername`, `ErrInvalidMethod`.\n*What would change my mind:* it is added to S1 with its trigger conditions enumerated.\n\n**8. `Country` is upper-cased on the way *in* to the query but nothing upper-cases it on the way in to the *table*.**\nAn `idp_cache` write storing `us` is invisible to a search for `US`. Exact match makes this silent.\n*What would change my mind:* a `CHECK (country = upper(country) AND length(country) = 2)` on the column, normalized at write in both backends."} </teammate-message> This came from another Claude session — not typed by your user, but very likely working on their behalf. Sep 12, 2:28 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
This is the highest-value round of the phase. Eight objections, all actionable, and at least four are real defects rather than preferences. **The two that would have shipped as bugs:** *Collation (#1).* PostgreSQL sorts `ORDER BY name` by database collation; SQLite's default is `BINARY` byte order, where every uppercase letter precedes every lowercase one. `Zebra` before `apple` on one backend, after it on the other. Anders' `id` tiebreak makes the order *total* but not *identical* — so under offset pagination, page 1 is a different set of rows depending on the backend. This is the `LIKE` finding in a second location, and I hadn't considered it. *The index doesn't match the predicate (#2).* Anders folds case with `LOWER(name) LIKE …`; the schema declares a trigram GIN index on the bare `name` column. A GIN index on the bare column isn't used for a predicate on `lower(name)`, so the accelerator accelerates nothing and both backends full-scan. The parity gap would close by making PostgreSQL as slow as SQLite — the fix for one problem silently creating another. This is the one I said I'd add myself if he missed it; he reached it independently, so the pattern found it rather than me routing around the pattern. *Unescaped `LIKE` input (#3)* — a search for `50%` matches every row, and the escape character itself differs between the two engines. *Country normalization (#8)* — `Country` is upper-cased going into the query but nothing upper-cases it going into the table, so an IDP write storing `us` is silently invisible to a search for `US`. **And one meta-objection I particularly rate:** on #4 he says plainly that nothing would change his mind, because he objects to *the silence, not the choice*. Rejecting empty-string filters means no legal query can find rows with a missing `region` — which is fine as a decision and not fine as an omission, given that partial addresses from `/identity` are the normal case. An objection that names which kind of disagreement it is saves a round trip. I've asked Anders whether objections 1, 2 and 8 share a general principle worth writing into the contract — they're all the same shape, a guarantee stated at the interface that the DDL doesn't enforce. If there's a single statement covering that class, it's worth more to 03 than eight individual patches. Awaiting his answers, then I rule. Beatriz's Table B is still outstanding.

Accepted, revised — and he is right that this was load-bearing.** My proposal specified a predicate the index as written cannot serve; the accelerator would have accelerated nothing. Revision: the index is an expression index, `GIN (LOWER(name) gin_trgm_ops)`, and S1's index list is corrected accordingly. Additional constraint the contract must state: the predicate expression has to match the index expression textually, so `LOWER(name) LIKE ?` is the only permitted form — a future `name ILIKE ?` silently loses the index.\n\n**3.   "content": "# Decision: the `Search()` query shape\n\nPattern: **adversarial pair** (`../../CASTING.md` §5, named for this decision by name). Decision owner: Priya Nandakumar, 05-data-ops. Story: S2 in `../PLAN.md`. Feeds: S1.\n\n**Question.** The `ProfileQuery` and result shape for `ProfileRepository.Search()` — field set, per-field match semantics, filter combination, pagination, sort, what `total` counts, and how the SQLite parity gap surfaces to a caller.\n\n**Why it was worth three turns.** `Search()` is the flagship query of this contract and the only genuinely open design question in it. Every other DAO method is settled by the assignment text. This one had the most ways to be quietly wrong, and eight of them were found.\n\n## Ruling: adopt the revised proposal\n\nProposer: Anders Vogel. Skeptic: Yusuf Karadag, eight numbered objections. **All eight accepted and revised; none declined.** That is an unusually clean sweep, and it is a fact about the objections rather than about the proposer — six of the eight identified something the proposal asserted but did not enforce.\n\n```go\ntype ProfileQuery struct {\n    Name    *string // optional: case-folded substring match, caller input escaped\n    Phone   *string // optional: exact match on the E.164-normalized value\n    Region  *string // optional: case-folded exact match\n    Country *string // optional: exact match, ISO 3166-1 alpha-2, upper-cased by the DAO\n    Limit   int     // 1..100; 0 means \"use default 50\", deliberately\n    Offset  int     // 0..10_000\n}\n\nSearch(ctx context.Context, q ProfileQuery) (results []UserProfile, total int, err error)\n```\n\n**Settled semantics:**\n\n- **Absent vs. empty.** `nil` = absent (don't filter). Non-nil but empty-after-trim is a caller bug → `ErrInvalidQuery`. Coercing `\"\"` to \"match everything\" is how a full-table scan reaches production.\n- **Combination.** AND across present filters. No OR, no negation.\n- **Empty query.** Legal, returns everything paginated. Whether a caller *may* do that is authorization policy owned by `../../02-ai-security-architecture/`; the DAO is the wrong layer to invent one, and the pagination ceiling is protection, not a policy veto.\n- **Case folding.** `LOWER(column) LIKE LOWER(pattern)`, applied explicitly in both implementations. Residual accepted gap: PostgreSQL's `lower()` is locale-aware, SQLite's is ASCII-only, so non-ASCII folding still diverges. Narrow and named, unlike the ASCII divergence it replaces.\n- **Escaping.** The DAO escapes `%`, `_` and `\\` in the caller's term and emits an explicit `ESCAPE '\\'` on **both** backends, rather than relying on PostgreSQL's default and SQLite's absence of one.\n- **Collation and sort.** `ORDER BY LOWER(name) COLLATE \"C\" ASC, id ASC`, fixed, not caller-configurable, with collation named in DDL as well — `COLLATE \"C\"` on PostgreSQL, `BINARY` on SQLite. Stated cost, not hidden: `C` is byte order, so non-English names sort in an order a human would call wrong. **Identical-across-backends is chosen over locale-correct**, because a POC that returns different page-1 rows per backend is broken in a way that a POC sorting diacritics oddly is not.\n- **Trigram index is an accelerator, never a ranker.** It accelerates the same `LIKE` predicate; no `similarity()` scoring, no rank ordering. This is what keeps the backends' row sets and order identical for ASCII input and confines the parity gap to speed. Adding ranking re-opens this decision — it does not get added quietly inside the `postgres` package.\n- **Index.** Expression index `GIN (LOWER(name) gin_trgm_ops)`, not the bare column. **The predicate expression must match the index expression textually**, so `LOWER(name) LIKE ?` is the only permitted form; a future `name ILIKE ?` silently loses the index.\n- **Pagination.** Limit/offset. Max page 100, default 50, offset ceiling 10 000. `Limit < 0` or `> 100`, and `Offset < 0` or `> 10_000`, return `ErrInvalidQuery`. Keyset rejected as designing for an uncommitted dataset size. Stated cost: offset pages are unstable under concurrent inserts — a row can shift or repeat across pages.\n- **`total`.** Exact count of all matching rows, ignoring limit/offset, via a second `COUNT(*)` in no wrapping transaction, so it may disagree with the page by a row under concurrent writes. **`total` is a magnitude, not a promise of reachability** — it can exceed what the offset ceiling allows a caller to page to.\n- **`Country`.** `CHECK (country = upper(country) AND length(country) = 2)` plus write-side normalization in both backends. SQLite's `upper()` is ASCII-only — sufficient for A–Z alpha-2 codes, written down so nobody assumes it generalizes.\n- **Out of scope, stated rather than left silent:** searching for *absent* address values. `nil` means don't filter and `\"\"` is an error, so no legal query reaches rows with a NULL `region`. Callers needing that list and filter client-side. This matters more than it sounds — partial addresses from `/identity` are the normal case for `idp_cache` rows, not an edge case.\n- **Transactions.** `Search()` is read-only, two independent statements, no explicit transaction, so it never encounters a client-retryable `40001`. The CockroachDB retry seam (`../research-cockroachdb-postgres-semantics.md`) is scoped to `Update()`, which is S1's problem.\n- **Sentinels.** `ErrInvalidQuery` joins the S1 set, triggers enumerated: empty-after-trim filter, `Limit` out of range, `Offset` negative or above ceiling, `Country` not two characters.\n\n## The general principle — the most valuable output of this run\n\nObjections 1, 2, 3 and 8 are one failure repeated: **a guarantee asserted at the interface with no enforcement site in the schema.** Interface prose enforces nothing.\n\n**Adopted into S1 as a requirement.** Every caller-visible guarantee in the contract carries a named enforcement site — a DDL constraint, an index definition, a normalization step, or a conformance test. *A guarantee with no site listed is not a guarantee, it is a hope.* S1 will carry this as a table, so the ninth instance is caught by the table rather than by a reviewer.\n\nThis generalizes the `LIKE` finding from S3 and both of this run's worst defects, and it is worth more to 03 than the eight individual patches are.\n\n## Lead's addition at synthesis\n\nOne point neither seat raised, added on my own authority rather than routed back for another turn: **no index supports the ordering.** A GIN trigram index cannot serve `ORDER BY`, so every `Search()` sorts its filtered set. Acceptable at this scale and for this POC, and explicitly *not* worth adding a second B-tree on `LOWER(name)` to fix — but it is a caller-visible performance characteristic and therefore, by the principle just adopted, needs its enforcement-site row to read \"none — accepted.\" A guarantee we are choosing not to make still gets written down.\n\n## Objections and their disposition\n\nAll eight objections, each closing with an explicit \"what would change my mind\" as `../../CASTING.md` §5 ⟨02⟩ requires, and all eight answered in writing. Full text in both hires' returned results. Disposition:\n\n| # | Objection | Disposition |\n|---|---|---|\n| 1 | `ORDER BY name` differs per backend (PG collation vs SQLite `BINARY` byte order); `id` tiebreak makes order total, not identical | **Accepted** — collation named in DDL and `ORDER BY` |\n| 2 | `LOWER(name) LIKE` cannot use a GIN index on the bare column; accelerator accelerates nothing | **Accepted** — expression index; predicate must match textually |\n| 3 | Caller input unescaped in `LIKE`; escape character differs across engines | **Accepted** — DAO escapes, explicit `ESCAPE '\\'` both backends |\n| 4 | No legal query can reach rows with a missing `region` | **Accepted as framed** — the objection was to the silence, not the choice; one line added |\n| 5 | `total` can report a count the offset ceiling forbids reaching | **Accepted** — `total` restated as a magnitude |\n| 6 | `Limit int` collapses \"unspecified\" and \"zero\"; negatives unaddressed | **Accepted** — validation rule stated; `int` retained over `*int` because `0` is not a meaningful page size and is therefore free as a sentinel, whereas `\"\"` is a meaningful string, which is why filters need pointers |\n| 7 | `ErrInvalidQuery` not in S1's sentinel set | **Accepted** — added with triggers |\n| 8 | `Country` upper-cased into the query but not into the table | **Accepted** — `CHECK` constraint plus write-side normalization |\n\nObjection 2 is the one I was independently watching for and deliberately did not feed to the Skeptic seat, to keep the pattern's integrity. He reached it unaided, which is evidence the seat is doing real work rather than confirming the lead's prior.\n\n---\n\n*Pattern footer (`../../CASTING.md` §5 ⟨02⟩). Written to the project's hand-off standard: **03 should be able to implement this without re-deriving my reasoning.** Where a decision has a rationale 03 might otherwise re-litigate, the rationale is here; where something is deliberately out of 03's scope, §6 says so and says why.\n\nEverything below is Go-*shaped* prose inside a markdown file. No `.go` or `.sql` files exist or should exist until Warren's explicit go-ahead.\n\nInputs that produced it: `decisions/search-query-shape.md` (S2), `decisions/multi-db-abstraction.md` (S3), `research-cockroachdb-postgres-semantics.md`, and a direct two-party agreement with 03 on the factory shape (below).\n\n## 1. The factory and the composite\n\nAgreed directly with Renata Cole (03) on 2026-09-12. **Her `dao.New` signature is unchanged**; the composite is the resolution of what had been a silent mismatch between her single `Repository` return type and this track's three interfaces.\n\n```go\nfunc New(driver, dsn string) (Repository, error)   // driver: \"postgres\" | \"cockroachdb\" | \"sqlite\"\n\ntype Repository interface {\n    Profiles()    ProfileRepository\n    Credentials() CredentialRepository\n    Methods()     AuthMethodRepository\n    Close() error\n}\n```\n\n`Repository` stays an **interface**, not a struct with exported fields — Renata's reason, which is better than either I offered: an interface is mockable for her test strategy. The three accessors stay distinct rather than flattening into one interface because that separation is a governance control (`pii-governance.md`), not a modelling preference: a call site must reach for the credential accessor deliberately rather than find profile and credential methods side by side on one value.\n\n`\"postgres\"` and `\"cockroachdb\"` both select the `postgres` package. They are distinct driver strings rather than one because the retry seam in §4 needs to know which engine it is talking to.\n\n## 2. Domain types\n\n```go\ntype UserProfile struct {\n    ID            string     // UUID\n    Name          string     // required, never empty\n    Phone         *string    // nil if unknown; E.164 when present\n    StreetAddress *string\n    Locality      *string\n    Region        *string\n    PostalCode    *string\n    Country       *string    // ISO 3166-1 alpha-2, upper-case\n    Source        string     // \"direct\" | \"idp_cache\"\n    CreatedAt     time.Time\n    UpdatedAt     time.Time\n}\n\ntype UserCredential struct {\n    ID          string     // UUID\n    UserID      string     // FK -> user_profile.id\n    Username    string     // unique, case-folded\n    MethodID    string     // FK -> auth_method.id\n    Secret      []byte     // nil unless SecretState == SecretSet\n    SecretState string     // \"none\" | \"set\" | \"revoked\"\n    HashAlgo    *string    // non-nil iff SecretState == \"set\"\n    HashCost    *int       // non-nil iff SecretState == \"set\"\n    CreatedAt   time.Time\n    UpdatedAt   time.Time\n}\n\ntype AuthMethod struct {\n    ID             string\n    Name           string  // \"password\", \"passkey\", \"otp\", \"oidc\", ...\n    RequiresSecret bool\n    IsActive       bool\n    CreatedAt      time.Time\n}\n```\n\n**Pointers mean \"absent\", never \"empty\".** No field in this contract uses the empty string to mean absent. A `*string` that is non-nil and empty is invalid input, not a value — see §5.\n\n**`SecretState` is new and is the fix for a real defect.** The previous prose said `secret` was \"nullable, salted hash only\" and stopped. Yusuf Karadag's review found that at least three distinct states were collapsing into one null — the method requires no server-held secret (passkey/WebAuthn, where the private key is client-side), the secret is not yet set, or the secret was revoked — and that `auth_method.requires_secret` was consequently load-bearing on nothing. An explicit state column makes the three distinguishable and gives `requires_secret` something to be checked against.\n\n## 3. The interfaces\n\n```go\ntype ProfileRepository interface {\n    Get(ctx context.Context, id string) (*UserProfile, error)\n    Search(ctx context.Context, q ProfileQuery) (results []UserProfile, total int, err error)\n    Create(ctx context.Context, p *UserProfile) (*UserProfile, error)\n    Update(ctx context.Context, p *UserProfile) (*UserProfile, error)\n    Upsert(ctx context.Context, p *UserProfile) (*UserProfile, error)\n    Delete(ctx context.Context, id string) error\n}\n\ntype CredentialRepository interface {\n    GetByUsername(ctx context.Context, username string) (*UserCredential, error)\n    ListByUserID(ctx context.Context, userID string) ([]UserCredential, error)\n    Create(ctx context.Context, c *UserCredential) (*UserCredential, error)\n    Update(ctx context.Context, c *UserCredential) (*UserCredential, error)\n    Delete(ctx context.Context, id string) error\n}\n\ntype AuthMethodRepository interface {\n    Get(ctx context.Context, id string) (*AuthMethod, error)\n    GetByName(ctx context.Context, name string) (*AuthMethod, error)\n    List(ctx context.Context, activeOnly bool) ([]AuthMethod, error)\n}\n\ntype ProfileQuery struct {\n    Name    *string // case-folded substring match; caller input escaped by the DAO\n    Phone   *string // exact match on the E.164-normalized value\n    Region  *string // case-folded exact match\n    Country *string // exact match, alpha-2, upper-cased by the DAO\n    Limit   int     // 1..100; 0 means \"use default 50\", deliberately\n    Offset  int     // 0..10_000\n}\n```\n\n`CredentialRepository` never accepts or returns a `UserProfile` field, in either direction, including as an internal convenience struct. That is §6.1.\n\n**`Create` / `Update` / `Upsert` are three distinct methods, and this changes the earlier prose.** The previous version of this document described `Update` as \"implemented as a portable upsert\", which would mean `Update` on a missing row silently *creates* it. That is exactly the kind of surprise that produces a bug in a caller, so it is ruled out:\n\n- `Create` — insert. `ErrAlreadyExists` if the ID is taken.\n- `Update` — update an existing row by ID. **`ErrNotFound` if the row does not exist.** It never creates.\n- `Upsert` — the portable `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (id) DO UPDATE` form. Exists because the IDP hydration path genuinely does not know whether the row exists, which is a real caller rather than a hypothetical one. Any other caller should use `Create` or `Update`.\n\n`Context` is first on every method and cancellation is honoured. **No method holds a transaction across calls.** If the service layer needs a multi-statement transaction, that is a change to this contract — flag it to 05 rather than improvising a `Begin`/`Commit` pair inside 03.\n\n## 4. Error semantics\n\n```go\nvar (\n    ErrNotFound          = errors.New(\"dao: not found\")\n    ErrAlreadyExists     = errors.New(\"dao: already exists\")\n    ErrDuplicateUsername = errors.New(\"dao: duplicate username\")\n    ErrInvalidMethod     = errors.New(\"dao: unknown or inactive auth method\")\n    ErrInvalidQuery      = errors.New(\"dao: invalid query\")\n    ErrInvalidCredential = errors.New(\"dao: credential violates secret-state rules\")\n)\n```\n\nEvery backend returns the same sentinel for the same condition; the service layer never distinguishes `sql.ErrNoRows` from a SQLite-specific miss. Test with `errors.Is`. Anything not in this set is wrapped with `%w` and passed through.\n\n**Translation is by SQLSTATE code only — never by matching error message text or constraint names.** `23505` → `ErrDuplicateUsername` or `ErrAlreadyExists` depending on the constrained column; `23503` → `ErrInvalidMethod`. This is not stylistic: `research-cockroachdb-postgres-semantics.md` §3 records that CockroachDB has a documented history of SQLSTATE inconsistency on constraint errors, and that message and `DETAIL` structure are not portable between the engines. String-matching an error message is fragile against one engine and reckless against two. Pin the CockroachDB target version.\n\n**`ErrInvalidQuery` triggers**, enumerated so 03 does not have to infer them: a non-nil filter that is empty after trimming; `Limit < 0` or `> 100`; `Offset < 0` or `> 10_000`; `Country` not exactly two characters.\n\n### The write-path retry seam — a decision, not an observation\n\nCockroachDB defaults to SERIALIZABLE and returns client-retryable `40001` in situations PostgreSQL at READ COMMITTED never produces, and its internal retry cannot help once results have begun streaming. `Upsert`'s `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` under contention is directly exposed to this.\n\n**Ruling: every write goes through a `crdb.ExecuteTx`-style retry wrapper, and the CockroachDB backend stays at its default SERIALIZABLE.** The alternative — running CockroachDB at READ COMMITTED to drop the requirement — was considered and rejected: CockroachDB's READ COMMITTED is documented as *stronger* than PostgreSQL's, so choosing it to \"simplify\" would introduce a second engine-behaviour divergence hiding behind a matching level name. That is the same error, one layer down, that this phase has spent its time removing. The wrapper is near-zero cost on PostgreSQL and load-bearing on CockroachDB.\n\n`Search` is read-only, two independent statements, no explicit transaction — it never encounters `40001` and needs no wrapper.\n\n## 5. Field-level schema\n\nNullability is the most expensive thing here to get wrong, which is why it carries its own column and its own reviewer.\n\n### `user_profile`\n\n| Column | Go type | Postgres / CockroachDB | SQLite | Null? | Constraint / note |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| `id` | `string` | `UUID DEFAULT gen_random_uuid()` | `TEXT` | NOT NULL | PK. App-generated in Go before insert on SQLite; server-generated elsewhere. Callers never see the difference. |\n| `name` | `string` | `TEXT COLLATE \"C\"` | `TEXT COLLATE BINARY` | NOT NULL | `CHECK (length(trim(name)) > 0)`. Collation named explicitly — see §6.3. |\n| `phone` | `*string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NULL ok | E.164-normalized at write. `CHECK (phone IS NULL OR phone ~ '^\\+[1-9][0-9]{1,14}$')`; SQLite uses a `GLOB` equivalent. |\n| `street_address` | `*string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NULL ok | `CHECK (x IS NULL OR length(trim(x)) > 0)` — NULL or a real value, never `''`. |\n| `locality` | `*string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NULL ok | as above |\n| `region` | `*string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NULL ok | as above |\n| `postal_code` | `*string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NULL ok | as above |\n| `country` | `*string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NULL ok | `CHECK (country IS NULL OR (country = upper(country) AND length(country) = 2))` |\n| `source` | `string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NOT NULL | `CHECK (source IN ('direct','idp_cache'))`. Governs retention — `pii-governance.md`. |\n| `created_at` | `time.Time` | `TIMESTAMPTZ` | `TEXT` (RFC3339 UTC) | NOT NULL | |\n| `updated_at` | `time.Time` | `TIMESTAMPTZ` | `TEXT` (RFC3339 UTC) | NOT NULL | Touched on every write, including IDP re-hydration. Retention depends on this — `pii-governance.md`. |\n\n**All five address columns are nullable, deliberately.** Partial addresses from `/identity` are the normal case for `idp_cache` rows, not an edge case. The `CHECK` forbidding empty strings is what keeps \"absent\" single-valued: NULL is absent, `''` is invalid, and no code has to decide which of two representations it is looking at.\n\n### `user_credential`\n\n| Column | Go type | Postgres / CockroachDB | SQLite | Null? | Constraint / note |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| `id` | `string` | `UUID` | `TEXT` | NOT NULL | PK |\n| `user_id` | `string` | `UUID` | `TEXT` | NOT NULL | FK → `user_profile(id)` **ON DELETE CASCADE**. See §6.2. |\n| `username` | `string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NOT NULL | **UNIQUE on `lower(username)`**, not on the raw column. |\n| `method_id` | `string` | `UUID` | `TEXT` | NOT NULL | FK → `auth_method(id)`, RESTRICT |\n| `secret` | `[]byte` | `BYTEA` | `BLOB` | NULL ok | Salted hash only, never plaintext. |\n| `secret_state` | `string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NOT NULL | `CHECK (secret_state IN ('none','set','revoked'))` |\n| `hash_algo` | `*string` | `TEXT` | `TEXT` | NULL ok | 02 owns the value set. |\n| `hash_cost` | `*int` | `INTEGER` | `INTEGER` | NULL ok | Stored so work factors can be raised later without a schema change (NIST SP 800-63B). |\n| `created_at` | `time.Time` | `TIMESTAMPTZ` | `TEXT` | NOT NULL | |\n| `updated_at` | `time.Time` | `TIMESTAMPTZ` | `TEXT` | NOT NULL | |\n\n**Table-level `CHECK` tying the secret columns together:**\n```\nCHECK (\n  (secret_state = 'set'  AND secret IS NOT NULL AND hash_algo IS NOT NULL AND hash_cost IS NOT NULL)\n  OR\n  (secret_state IN ('none','revoked') AND secret IS NULL AND hash_algo IS NULL AND hash_cost IS NULL)\n)\n```\nThis is what makes a row with a secret and no algorithm, or an algorithm and no secret, unrepresentable rather than merely discouraged.\n\n**No PII columns exist on this table and none may be added.** §6.1.\n\n### `auth_method`\n\n| Column | Go type | Both families | Null? | Note |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| `id` | `string` | UUID / TEXT | NOT NULL | PK |\n| `name` | `string` | TEXT | NOT NULL | UNIQUE. Seeded with `'password'`. |\n| `requires_secret` | `bool` | BOOLEAN / INTEGER 0-1 | NOT NULL | `false` for passkey/WebAuthn-style methods |\n| `is_active` | `bool` | BOOLEAN / INTEGER 0-1 | NOT NULL | |\n| `created_at` | `time.Time` | TIMESTAMPTZ / TEXT | NOT NULL | |\n\n### Indexes\n\n| Index | Where | Serves |\n|---|---|---|\n| `GIN (LOWER(name) gin_trgm_ops)` | Postgres + CockroachDB only | `Search` name filter. **Expression index, not the bare column** — a GIN index on bare `name` is not used by a `LOWER(name)` predicate, so the accelerator would accelerate nothing. The predicate must match the index expression textually: `LOWER(name) LIKE ?` is the only permitted form, and a future `name ILIKE ?` silently loses the index. |\n| B-Tree on `phone` | all | `Search` phone filter (exact/prefix on normalized values) |\n| UNIQUE on `lower(username)` | all | auth lookup; case-folded uniqueness |\n| B-Tree on `user_credential(user_id)` | all | `ListByUserID`, cascade delete |\n| B-Tree on `user_credential(method_id)` | all | FK integrity |\n| *(none)* | — | **`Search` ordering has no supporting index.** GIN cannot serve `ORDER BY`, so every `Search` sorts its filtered set. Accepted at this scale; recorded because a guarantee we are choosing *not* to make still gets written down. |\n\n## 6. Out of scope for 03 — do not redesign; flag to 05 instead\n\nNumbered so there is no ambiguity. Each names **the enforcement site that makes it hold**, per the principle adopted in `decisions/search-query-shape.md`: *a guarantee with no named enforcement site is not a guarantee, it is a hope.*\n\n1. **PII/credential separation.** `user_credential` carries no name, phone or address; `user_profile` carries no secret. This is a governance control, not normalization convenience — a breach of one table must not expose the other class of data (`pii-governance.md`). *Enforcement: the column sets in §5, the three-accessor split in §1, and a conformance test asserting neither struct gains a field from the other.*\n2. **Cascade delete from profile to credential, and its asymmetry.** Deleting a profile destroys its credentials; the account ceases to exist. **The reverse does not hold** — deleting a credential leaves the profile and its PII standing on the `direct` retention clock. Deliberate. If \"removing your last credential deletes the account\" is wanted, that is a product policy decision, not a schema one, and it is currently *not* what the schema does. *Enforcement: `ON DELETE CASCADE` on the FK; the asymmetry is stated in `pii-governance.md`.*\n3. **Case, collation and folding.** `ORDER BY LOWER(name) COLLATE \"C\" ASC, id ASC`, fixed and not caller-configurable, with collation named in DDL as well — `COLLATE \"C\"` on Postgres, `BINARY` on SQLite. Without this, page 1 of a search returns *different rows* per backend, because Postgres sorts by database collation while SQLite's default byte order puts every uppercase letter before every lowercase one. Cost accepted and named: `C` is byte order, so non-English names sort in an order a human would call wrong — identical-across-backends beats locale-pretty for a system whose whole premise is one contract over three engines. *Enforcement: the DDL collation clause, the `ORDER BY` expression, and a conformance test asserting identical ordering across backends.*\n4. **`LIKE` escaping.** The DAO escapes `%`, `_` and `\\` in caller input and emits an explicit `ESCAPE '\\'` on both backends — Postgres has a default backslash escape, SQLite has none unless given. Without this, a search for `50%` matches every row. *Enforcement: the DAO's query construction, plus a conformance test using a term containing `%`.*\n5. **`Country` normalization at write, not only at read.** Upper-casing the query without upper-casing the table makes a stored `us` silently invisible to a search for `US`. SQLite's `upper()` is ASCII-only — sufficient for alpha-2 codes, written down so nobody assumes it generalizes. *Enforcement: the `CHECK` constraint in §5 plus write-side normalization in both implementations.*\n6. **UUID primary keys on every backend.** Required for CockroachDB (sequential keys create write hotspots on a single range), supported on Postgres, app-generated on SQLite. Settled by sourced research; do not reopen. *Enforcement: the column types in §5.*\n7. **`auth_method` as a lookup table, not a native enum.** Adding a method is an `INSERT`, not a migration and not a deploy. *Enforcement: the FK from `user_credential.method_id`; see `migration-approach.md` §4.*\n8. **The `ON CONFLICT ... DO UPDATE` upsert form.** The one syntax all three backends accept; CockroachDB's `UPSERT` shorthand is deliberately unused to avoid a second dialect. *Enforcement: `Upsert`'s single query shape.*\n9. **The two-package split** (`postgres` serving Postgres + CockroachDB, `sqlite` separate) and the engine-aware retry seam inside the former. *Enforcement: package boundaries; `decisions/multi-db-abstraction.md`.*\n10. **The `requires_secret` ↔ `secret_state` relationship.** A credential whose method has `requires_secret = false` must not carry `secret_state = 'set'`. **This one cannot be a DDL constraint** — `CHECK` cannot reference another table — so its enforcement site is DAO-layer validation returning `ErrInvalidCredential`, plus a conformance test. Named honestly as the weakest site in this contract, because the discipline is worthless if it only records the cases where a clean site existed.\n\n## 7. The conformance suite — a requirement, not a suggestion\n\n**One suite, written once against the interface, executed against every backend implementation.** Not per-package unit tests: the same assertions, run twice, so a behavioural difference fails a test rather than reaching a user.\n\nThis is the condition the whole per-driver ruling rests on (`decisions/multi-db-abstraction.md`). Two implementations behind one interface guarantee both *compile*; nothing guarantees they *behave* alike, and the failure mode produces no error — just different results. Every defect this phase found (the `LIKE` case divergence, the collation ordering divergence, `Country` normalization) is invisible when either implementation is read in isolation, because each is correct on its own terms.\n\nMechanism is 03's. The requirement is 05's, and at minimum the suite asserts: identical result sets and identical ordering for the same `Search` across backends; `errors.Is` sentinel parity for every error condition in §4; the secret-state rules in §5 and §6.10; and empty-string rejection on every `*string` filter.\n\n---\n\n*AI tooling note: this contract was produced by Claude Opus 5 (05 track-lead session) synthesizing two orchestrated debates run with four Claude Sonnet 5 hires — a three-hats run on the abstraction and an adversarial pair on the query shape, artifacts under `decisions/` — plus one lead-authorized Sonnet research pass (`research-cockroachdb-postgres-semantics.md`) and a direct two-party agreement with the 03 track lead on the factory shape. Six of the eleven substantive changes in it came from hires contradicting the lead's draft, which is what the seats were bought for.*\nDOCEOF\necho \"appended\"; grep -c \"\" /Users/warrenthompson/Source/LoginID/05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md",

## 6. What changes if the abstraction ruling changes

Story S3 in `./PLAN.md` — the ruling on the multi-database abstraction shape — was an open three-hats debate, including codegen (sqlc) as a real option that has been name-checked but never argued here. For honesty about what is settled: if codegen wins, the DAO's query layer changes and 03's work changes with it — **but migrations do not.** sqlc generates Go from SQL; it does not manage schema versions. The layout, the two-invocation mechanism, R1–R5, and the seed-data rule survive that ruling intact. Nothing in this document is waiting on S3.

Sep 12, 2:34 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
S1 is drafted and out for cold read. The contract is the substantial piece of this track, so here's what's actually in it. **Three corrections to my own document, made in place with the reasoning visible** rather than quietly edited: the SQLite parity gap (performance → correctness), the dialect-branch rejection reasoning (two conditionals, not a scattering), and the shared-`postgres`-package justification (now names the retry seam it requires). **Four decisions I made as lead that go beyond what the debates settled:** *`Update` no longer creates.* The earlier prose had `Update` implemented as a portable upsert, which means `Update` on a missing row silently inserts it. That's a bug generator. It's now three distinct methods — `Create`, `Update` (returns `ErrNotFound`), and `Upsert` for the IDP hydration path, which is a real caller that genuinely doesn't know whether the row exists. *The write-path retry seam.* Every write goes through a retry wrapper with CockroachDB at its default SERIALIZABLE. I rejected the simpler alternative — running CockroachDB at READ COMMITTED to drop the requirement — because CockroachDB's READ COMMITTED is documented as *stronger* than PostgreSQL's, so picking it to simplify would hide a second engine divergence behind a matching level name. That's the same error this phase has spent its time removing, one layer down. *`secret_state` as a new column.* Yusuf's finding that three states were collapsing into one null is fixed properly — an explicit `'none'|'set'|'revoked'` state with a table-level `CHECK` tying it to `secret`, `hash_algo` and `hash_cost`. A row with a secret and no algorithm is now unrepresentable rather than merely discouraged, and `requires_secret` finally has something to be checked against. *Empty string is never a value.* Every optional field is `*string`, NULL means absent, and a `CHECK` forbids `''`. One representation of absence, so no code has to decide which it's looking at. **The out-of-scope list names an enforcement site for every item** — and item 10 names the weakest one honestly: the `requires_secret` ↔ `secret_state` rule can't be a DDL constraint because `CHECK` can't reference another table, so it's DAO validation plus a conformance test. The discipline is worthless if it only records the cases where a clean site existed. Wesley's cold read on the migration doc came back almost entirely as undefined vocabulary — `goose`, `pg_trgm`, "advisory lock", "type affinity" — all fixed. One wasn't cosmetic: he couldn't tell whether `pg_trgm` was background or actionable, and it's actionable, because it's why the migration runner needs `CREATE EXTENSION` on PostgreSQL and nothing on CockroachDB. If he couldn't connect that, Theo would have provisioned one credential for both. Outstanding: Saoirse's cold read, Beatriz's Table B and her S4 objection turn.

Together they make `LIKE '%smith%'` use an index instead of scanning every row.\n- **`COLLATE \"C\"`** — tells the database to compare text by raw byte value rather than by language-aware alphabetical rules. Byte order sorts all uppercase before all lowercase; a locale-aware order does not. Picking one explicitly is what makes two different engines agree.\n- **SERIALIZABLE / READ COMMITTED** — transaction isolation levels. SERIALIZABLE is stricter and can force the database to reject a transaction that would produce an inconsistent result, asking the client to retry.\n- **`crdb.ExecuteTx`** — a helper from CockroachDB's official Go library that runs a transaction and automatically retries it when the database returns the retryable `40001` error. This is not stylistic: `research-cockroachdb-postgres-semantics.md` §3 records that CockroachDB has a documented history of SQLSTATE inconsistency on constraint errors, and that message and `DETAIL` structure are not portable between the engines. String-matching an error message is fragile against one engine and reckless against two. Pin the CockroachDB target version.\"\"\",\n\"\"\"### How driver errors become sentinels\n\nThe first draft said \"translation is by SQLSTATE code only — never by matching error message text or constraint names.\" A cold read found that rule both **self-contradicting** (`23505` alone cannot say *which* column collided, yet the same paragraph asked for different sentinels per column) and **unimplementable on half the codebase** (SQLite has no SQLSTATE at all). Both were right. The revised rule:\n\n**Match on structured error codes, plus constraint names that we ourselves define in our own DDL. Never parse free-text error messages.**\n\n- *Postgres / CockroachDB*: match SQLSTATE, then disambiguate within it by the constraint name the error carries.\n- *SQLite*: match the extended result code (`SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_UNIQUE`, `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_FOREIGNKEY`, `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_CHECK`), then disambiguate by the same constraint name.\n\nConstraint names are pinned **because they are our artifact, not the engine's**: we write them in our own migrations, so they are stable across engines and versions in a way an engine-generated message is not. That is the distinction the original rule was reaching for and stated wrongly. Every constraint in §5 is explicitly named in DDL for this reason; an unnamed constraint is a translation failure waiting to happen.\n\n| Condition | PG / CRDB | SQLite | Sentinel |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| duplicate username | `23505` on `uq_user_credential_username` | `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_UNIQUE`, same name | `ErrDuplicateUsername` |\n| duplicate id | `23505` on a PK constraint | `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_PRIMARYKEY` | `ErrAlreadyExists` |\n| unknown `method_id` | `23503` on `fk_user_credential_method` | `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_FOREIGNKEY` | `ErrInvalidMethod` |\n| any CHECK violation (`source`, `name`, `phone`, `country`, address non-empty) | `23514` | `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_CHECK` | `ErrInvalidArgument` |\n| secret-state CHECK | `23514` on `ck_user_credential_secret_state` | same, same name | `ErrInvalidCredential` |\n\n**`ErrInvalidArgument` closes a real hole**: the commonest caller mistake is a write that violates a CHECK constraint, and in the first draft that arrived at the service layer as a raw, engine-specific driver error. `ErrInvalidQuery` protected reads and nothing protected writes.\n\n`research-cockroachdb-postgres-semantics.md` §3 records that CockroachDB has a documented history of SQLSTATE inconsistency on constraint errors and that message/`DETAIL` structure is not portable — so pin the CockroachDB target version, and treat any message-text matching found in review as a defect.\"\"\"))\n\n# ErrInvalidMethod enforcement site for \"inactive\"\nreps.append((\"\"\"10. **The `requires_secret` ↔ `secret_state` relationship.**\"\"\",\n\"\"\"10. **`is_active` on `auth_method`.** `ErrInvalidMethod` is documented as \"unknown **or inactive**\", and the foreign key catches only *unknown* — a cold read applied item 11's own standard back to this document and found it failing its test. `is_active = false` has no DDL enforcement site and cannot have one, for the same reason as item 11: a `CHECK` on `user_credential` cannot read `auth_method`. *Enforcement: the DAO checks `is_active` before insert and returns `ErrInvalidMethod`, plus a conformance test that creates a credential against a deactivated method and asserts the sentinel.* Deactivating a method deliberately does **not** invalidate existing credentials — it prevents new ones.\n11. **The `requires_secret` ↔ `secret_state` relationship.**\"\"\"))\n\n# phone GLOB equivalent\nreps.append((\"\"\"`CHECK (phone IS NULL OR phone ~ '^\\\\+[1-9][0-9]{1,14}$')`; SQLite uses a `GLOB` equivalent.\"\"\",\n\"\"\"named `ck_user_profile_phone_e164`. Postgres/CockroachDB: `CHECK (phone IS NULL OR phone ~ '^\\\\+[1-9][0-9]{1,14}$')`. SQLite has no regex, so: `CHECK (phone IS NULL OR (phone GLOB '+[1-9]*' AND length(phone) BETWEEN 8 AND 16 AND NOT phone GLOB '*[^+0-9]*'))`. That is §6.1.\"\"\",\n\"\"\"### `Search` semantics carried over from the S2 ruling\n\nThese were settled in `decisions/search-query-shape.md` and were missing from the first draft of this contract — the intent-versus-text gap a cold read was run to find.\n\n- **`total` is the count of ALL rows matching the filters, ignoring `Limit` and `Offset`** — not the number of rows on this page. Exact, not estimated, computed by a second `COUNT(*)` outside any wrapping transaction, so under concurrent writes it may disagree with the page by a row. **`total` is a magnitude, not a promise of reachability**: it can legitimately exceed what the `Offset` ceiling of 10 000 allows a caller to page to.\n- **An all-nil `ProfileQuery` is legal** and returns every profile, paginated. `ErrInvalidQuery` covers an *empty* filter (a non-nil pointer to `\"\"`), not the *absence* of filters. Whether a given caller may list all profiles is authorization policy owned by `../02-ai-security-architecture/`; the DAO does not invent one, and the pagination ceiling is protection rather than a policy veto.\n- **Filters combine with AND.** No OR, no negation.\n- **Searching for *absent* address values is out of scope.** `nil` means \"don't filter\" and `\"\"` is an error, so no legal query reaches rows with a NULL `region`. Callers needing that list and filter client-side. This matters because partial addresses from `/identity` are the normal case for `idp_cache` rows.\n\n### What `Search` does on SQLite — read this before assuming parity\n\nThe first draft said nothing about this anywhere; the only trace was \"Postgres + CockroachDB only\" in the index table, which a reader cannot turn into behaviour.\n\nSQLite runs **the same query with the same results in the same order** — `LOWER(name) LIKE ?` with explicit case folding and `COLLATE BINARY` ordering — but **without any index to accelerate it**, so it is a full table scan. Row sets and ordering are identical to Postgres for ASCII input; only speed differs. That identity is not an accident of the query text, it is asserted by the conformance suite in §7, which is the only thing that keeps it true as the code changes.\n\nThe one residual difference, accepted and named: Postgres `lower()` is locale-aware and SQLite's is ASCII-only, so **non-ASCII** case folding still diverges between backends. SQLite is the local/dev/demo backend, not a production peer.\n\n`CredentialRepository` never accepts or returns a `UserProfile` field, in either direction, including as an internal convenience struct. That is §6.1.\"\"\"))\n\n# Index table: phone exact only\nreps.append((\"| B-Tree on `phone` | all | `Search` phone filter (exact/prefix on normalized values) |\",\n\"| B-Tree on `phone` | all | `Search` phone filter. **Exact match only** — the `ProfileQuery.Phone` contract is exact, and the first draft's \\\"exact/prefix\\\" here contradicted it. Patch semantics are read-modify-write at the caller.* She called this the single most consequential gap and was right.\n\n2. **The error-translation rule was self-contradicting and half-unimplementable.** The contract said \"translation is by SQLSTATE code only — never by constraint names,\" then immediately asked for different sentinels for `23505` depending on which column collided, which the code alone cannot say. Worse: **SQLite has no SQLSTATE at all**, so the rule was unimplementable across half the codebase. *Ruled: match structured codes per engine (SQLSTATE on PG/CRDB, extended result codes on SQLite), disambiguated by constraint names **we define in our own DDL** — our artifact, stable across engines, unlike an engine-generated message. Full mapping table added; every constraint in §5 now explicitly named.*\n\n3. **`Upsert` could not serve the caller it was justified with.** The contract introduced `Upsert` for the IDP hydration path, which \"does not know whether the row exists\" — but `Upsert` conflicts on `id`, and a caller that doesn't know the row exists doesn't know its `id` either. There is no unique key on phone or name. *Ruled: the fix is deletion of the claim, not explanation. `Upsert` is for callers that already hold an `id`; resolving an `/identity` payload to an existing profile is explicitly the connector's problem.*\n\n4. **No sentinel for write-side validation.** `ErrInvalidQuery` protected reads; nothing protected writes, so the commonest caller mistake — violating a CHECK constraint on `source`, `name`, `phone` or `country` — reached the service layer as a raw, engine-specific driver error. *Ruled: `ErrInvalidArgument` added, with `23514` / `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_CHECK` mapped to it.*\n\n### The item that applied this document's own standard back at it\n\n5. **`ErrInvalidMethod` is documented as \"unknown *or inactive*\", and the FK catches only *unknown*.** `is_active = false` had no enforcement site, in a numbered list whose entire premise is that every guarantee names one. *Ruled: added as §6 item 10, enforcement site named as DAO-layer validation plus a conformance test, and flagged as one of the two weakest sites in the contract rather than quietly patched.* The standard catching its own author is the strongest evidence available that it works.\n\n### The remaining nine, all accepted\n\n`Create` and the `ID` field contradicted each other (§2 said the DAO generates it, §3 returned `ErrAlreadyExists` \"if the ID is taken\") — ruled: caller passes `\"\"`, non-empty is `ErrInvalidArgument`. Ownership of `CreatedAt`/`UpdatedAt` unstated — ruled: DAO-owned, ignored on input. `total` never defined in the contract despite `PLAN.md` naming it as something S2 had to settle — the intent-versus-text gap; now defined as all matching rows ignoring limit/offset, and as a magnitude rather than a promise of reachability. `Phone` match semantics contradicted between the struct comment (\"exact\") and the index table (\"exact/prefix\") — ruled exact. All-nil `ProfileQuery` legality unstated — ruled legal. `Delete` on a missing row unstated — ruled `ErrNotFound`. `GetByUsername` case-folding ownership unstated — ruled DAO-side. The SQLite `GLOB` phone constraint was left as \"a GLOB equivalent\" — she correctly noted that inventing it is a design decision, not an implementation detail; it is now given in full.\n\n### Surprise-versus-warning audit\n\nShe was asked, for each of the contract's three counter-intuitive behaviours, whether a reader meets the surprise before or after the sentence warning them:\n\n| Behaviour | Verdict | Action |\n|---|---|---|\n| `Update` does not create | **Warned first**, immediately under the method block | none |\n| Cascade delete profile→credential | **Surprise ~140 lines before its explanation** at §6.2 | pointer added at the method list |\n| SQLite `Search` parity | **No warning at all** — the only trace was \"Postgres + CockroachDB only\" in the index table, which a reader cannot turn into behaviour | new subsection: same results, same order, no index, full scan; non-ASCII folding the named residual |\n\n### Tail findings, all adopted\n\n- **The retry seam ruled *that* writes retry, never *where* the wrapper sits.** Three implementers would produce three faithful, different codebases. Now settled: an unexported helper inside the `postgres` package; **no shim at all** in `sqlite` (a no-op would imply a shared abstraction that does not exist); not in the composite, which would wrap reads and push engine-specific behaviour above the interface boundary.\n- **The `\"postgres\"` / `\"cockroachdb\"` driver-string split asserted the seam \"needs to know which engine\" without saying how it finds out**, given both select the same package. Now: an unexported `engine` field on the implementation struct, which is the entire reason the two strings are distinct.\n- **The address rows collapsed four columns into one `CHECK (x ...)` plus three \"as above\"** — requiring the reader to substitute the column name four times, immediately above the row where the SQLite variant was genuinely missing. Her point: the reader is trained to fill in a blank just before reaching a blank they cannot fill. All four written out and named.\n- **Organized by artifact; implemented by verb.** Four of the fourteen blockers existed only because no single place assembled a method's full story. She raised this as a question rather than a rewrite request and said explicitly that \"no\" was a fine answer. *Adopted:* §3a, a per-method table — who supplies the ID, who stamps timestamps, replace-or-patch, errors returned — which closes those four by construction rather than one prose sentence at a time.\n- **On \"enforcement site\" as vocabulary**, she volunteered the opposite of a finding: it arrives self-defining and should *not* get a glossary entry. She also noted that §6's admission of its own weakest link reads as honesty rather than hedging only because the standard was stated first — the ordering is load-bearing.\n\n### Jargon undefined on first use\n\n`gin_trgm_ops`/GIN, `crdb.ExecuteTx`, SQLSTATE, `COLLATE \"C\"`, `40001`/`23505`/`23503`, SERIALIZABLE vs. READ COMMITTED — all now defined in a vocabulary block. Bare `S1`/`S2`/`S3` story shorthand used without expansion: **the second reader in a row to catch this**, after Wesley found it in `migration-approach.md`, which makes it a habit of the author's rather than an oversight. Fixed as a class in both documents.\n\n## Parallel cold read: `migration-approach.md` (Wesley Okonkwo, 04, on loan)\n\nSix of seven findings were undefined-on-first-use vocabulary: `goose`, `goose fix`, SQLite \"type affinity\", \"advisory lock\", and `pg_trgm`. All now defined inline.\n\nOne was not cosmetic. He could not tell whether `pg_trgm` was background or actionable — and it is actionable: it is precisely why the migration runner needs `CREATE EXTENSION` privileges on PostgreSQL and none on CockroachDB. Theo confirmed the consequence downstream: he had not broken the migration-runner credential out as its own row in `secrets-delivery.md`, and has now separated it from the runtime DML-only credential. **A glossary gap in this track's document had propagated into a missing row in another track's secrets inventory.**\n\nHe also flagged, against his own stated blind spot (mistaking a deliberate open item for a gap), that R2's \"tell me if the platform cannot guarantee single-runner exclusion\" read as a genuinely open question handed to 04. It was, and it now says so in bold.\n\n## What this pass establishes\n\nThe hand-off standard this track asked the project to adopt is testable, and the test fails on a document written by the person who proposed the standard. That is the useful result. A contract reviewed only by people who watched it being written is reviewed for correctness, not for legibility, and those are different properties — every one of the fourteen items was invisible to four readers who already knew what the document meant.\n\n**Recommendation for any future run of this seat: keep the reader cold, and accept the cost.** Saoirse's own closing assessment is the argument: she cannot say which of the fourteen she would still have found had she read the debates first, and that uncertainty is exactly why the restriction is worth its price.\n\n---\n\n*Pattern footer (`../../CASTING.md` §5 ⟨02⟩). Sep 12, 2:41 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
Oren's review found four things, and two of them mattered a lot. **#4 was a genuine hole in my contract, not an ambiguity in it.** I barred 03 from composing a cross-call transaction, then supplied no method that writes more than one table — so the assignment's registration flow had *no atomic path at all*. Two independent calls leave a window where a profile exists with PII and no credential, and if the second call fails that window never closes. The contract was internally consistent and jointly unsatisfiable with the thing it exists to support. What makes it worth noting: **that orphan is the same failure class Beatriz found in retention** — a PII-bearing row nothing references, governed by a clock written for a different case. Her version was the IDP cache; this is direct registration. Two hires working on unrelated deliverables found the same shape twice, which suggests it's structural rather than incidental. Ruled by adding one method to the composite: `CreateProfileWithCredential`, both rows in one transaction, inside the retry seam on CockroachDB, `c.UserID` ignored on input so a caller can't supply a mismatched pair. It goes on the composite rather than `CredentialRepository` specifically so §6.1 holds — that interface still never touches a `UserProfile`. It's the only cross-table operation in the contract and a second one requires changing the contract, not an implementation decision. **#3 would have shipped silently.** `Upsert`'s `DO UPDATE SET` list didn't explicitly exclude `created_at`, so every IDP re-hydration would have reset the creation timestamp — on the exact column retention keys to. No test catches that unless someone thinks to look. On **#2** I declined to change the design and said so: `Limit == 0` meaning "default" *is* the same zero-value trap the pointer convention avoids elsewhere, and I'd rather name the inconsistency than pretend it isn't one. Requesting zero rows is a different operation and would arrive as an explicit `Count()`, not a magic value. On **#1** the useful ruling was to tell 03 *not* to test something: `ErrAlreadyExists` stays translated but is documented as an internal-error signal, since no caller can reach it. Four separate reviewers have now improved this contract — a Systems Cartographer, a Detail Hawk, a cold reader, and 03's own Detail Hawk — and each found a distinct class of defect the others didn't. Still outstanding: Beatriz's Table B and her S4 objection turn.

Sep 12, 2:42 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
**S5 is closed.** Beatriz's retention work is in `pii-governance.md`, and it required a schema addition I've now made: a `deletion_log` table, PII-free by construction, with `profile_id` deliberately carrying **no foreign key** — the row it names is meant not to exist. It's §6 item 11 in the contract, with a conformance test asserting no PII-shaped column ever appears on it. Her own line is why: otherwise the log is just a second copy of the problem with no clock on it. The strongest thing in her work is the health check, and it's the part I'd have been least likely to write myself: > A deletion job that stops running produces **no error, no alert and no visible symptom**. The only evidence is data that should be absent and isn't. So the sweep emits rows examined, rows deleted, and **the age of the oldest surviving row per class** — and that third metric is the actual health check. 04 alerts when it exceeds the class's window plus one sweep interval. Rows-deleted counts cannot detect a job that has silently stopped; only the age of what survives can. That goes to Theo as a requirement. Two other rulings worth recording. **Hard delete, not a soft-delete flag** — "a flag means the PII is still sitting in the table and every query written from that day forward must remember to filter it out; that is how a retention policy becomes decorative while still passing its own tests." And **subject deletion reuses the sweep's exact code path**, because a separate rarely-used "real delete" path is a path that's broken when you need it. Her orphan-clock finding also turned out to be half of a general rule rather than a special case. The 30-day cache window measures time since *last use*, not since first seen, because re-hydration moves `updated_at` — which is correct, and is precisely why the orphan row is keyed on `created_at` instead. A row still in use should survive; a row that can refresh itself by being re-read should not become immortal. Both are now presented as one rule seen from two sides. Track status: S1, S2, S3, S5, S6 closed. **S4 is the only open item** — one objection turn from Beatriz, then the final migration document goes to Theo.

  "content": "# Decision: objection turn on the migration approach\n\nPattern: **rotating devil's advocate** (`../../CASTING.md` §5 — the cheapest pattern in the catalog, meant to be run often). Objection seat: Beatriz Achterberg (Spreadsheet). Decision owner: Priya Nandakumar. Story: S4 in `../PLAN.md`. Subject: `../migration-approach.md`, the 05→04 hand-off.\n\nSix objections raised, **six accepted**. One of them overturned the reasoning behind the document's central requirement.\n\n## 1. R2's conclusion no longer followed from its own corrected premise — accepted\n\nThe strongest objection, and the one I would have shipped without.\n\nR2 requires that exactly one migration runner execute at a time, enforced by the orchestration platform rather than the database. The original argument was: \"a locking strategy that only works on one of three backends is not a strategy.\" That was true of the premise as first written — that CockroachDB has no advisory locks at all. **But I had already corrected that premise**, in `../research-cockroachdb-postgres-semantics.md` §5: transaction-scoped `pg_advisory_xact_lock` *does* work on CockroachDB. Session-scoped locks error, and the `pg_try_*` variants silently no-op.\n\nThe objection: with the corrected facts, a transaction-scoped lock works on **both** backends that can actually have replicas — SQLite being a single-file local backend where two concurrent migration runners is not a scenario that exists. I had corrected the premise and left the conclusion resting on the old one, which is a worse failure than never correcting it: the document read as though it were reasoning from evidence it had already superseded.\n\n**Ruling: the requirement stands; the reasoning is replaced.** A transaction-scoped advisory lock is released when its transaction ends, and goose applies each migration file in its own transaction — so the lock would be released *between files*, letting a second runner interleave mid-run. That is exactly the failure it was meant to prevent. Session-scoped locks would span the whole run, and those are the ones CockroachDB does not support. **The database can offer the right scope or the right engine coverage, never both.**\n\nThe objection anticipated that a good reason existed and said so explicitly — \"*Closes if:* you show the transaction-scoped lock is impractical for a different reason… That is a good reason. It is not the reason written down.\" That is the distinction between an objection and a complaint, and it is why this seat is worth one turn per decision.\n\n**This is the third time in this track that a hire has left a conclusion standing and destroyed the reasoning under it** (after the S3 dialect-branch count and the S2 error-translation rule). Three times is a pattern rather than three incidents: conclusions here have generally been right, and the arguments offered for them have generally been the first plausible one to hand rather than the true one.\n\n## 2. The cheapest backstop was never examined — accepted\n\nThe document asserted the database could not help, without examining the one database-level mechanism goose itself creates: its version table, the single artifact all three backends share. A unique constraint on `version_id` would make a second concurrent runner fail on insert — portable, free, no platform feature required.\n\n**Ruling: accepted, and recorded with its gap visible.** Whether goose's default version table already carries that constraint is **unverified**, and the document now says so rather than asserting either way. The actionable form does not depend on the answer: add a unique index on `version_id` in the first migration, which costs nothing if goose already does it. It is a backstop, not a replacement for R2 — it converts a silent interleave into a loud failure, which is the property worth buying.\n\nLeaving this as an explicit \"unverified\" rather than resolving it was deliberate. The objection's own framing — an empty cell is a question, not a gap to paper over — applies to the author as much as to the tables she wrote.\n\n## 3. The tool-independence claim had a counterexample in its own document — accepted\n\n`migration-approach.md` claimed \"nothing in §3–§6 changes if the tool changes.\" But §2's two-invocation mechanism uses goose's `-table` flag to keep two version sequences apart; swapping to golang-migrate means re-deriving it. Minor in effect, but it is the claim's only load-bearing use. **Ruling: accepted; the exception is now named inline.** An overclaim in a document whose entire argument is about not overclaiming would have been a poor advertisement for the rest of it.\n\n## 4. R3 checked one version number where §2 creates two — accepted\n\nFail-closed said the service compares \"the applied version\" against the version it was built against. §2 creates **two** independent sequences — `shared/` and the backend directory. As written, a service could pass the check with `shared/` current and the `pg_trgm` index unapplied: a passing check that proves nothing, which is worse than no check.\n\n**Ruling: accepted.** R3 now requires comparing both, and knowing which backend directory applies to the engine it is connected to.\n\n## 5. `goose fix` had no stated cutoff — accepted\n\nThe document said to renumber \"before anything is tagged.\" The real constraint is earlier and different: renumbering after migrations have been applied anywhere persistent rewrites history the version table already records. **Ruling: accepted** — it is a pre-first-application step, not a tidy-up before tagging.\n\n## 6. A gap the objector's own other deliverable created — accepted\n\n`deletion_log`, introduced by the retention mechanism in `../pii-governance.md` (S5), is schema, and §2's `shared/` contents list did not include it. Caught by the hire who had written the table that created the gap, in the course of attacking a different document.\n\n**Ruling: accepted;** `shared/` now lists it. Worth recording because it is the argument for running the objection seat *after* the sibling deliverable rather than in parallel with it — a cross-deliverable inconsistency is invisible to anyone holding only one of the two.\n\n## Also recorded: an unmodelled area, flagged by the objector against her own blind spot\n\nNot an objection to this document, but raised alongside it and worth keeping: **subject-deletion requests reach `user_profile` and its cascade, and do not reach derived data** — logs, metrics, connector traces that may have incidentally captured PII in transit. That data has no `source` column, no retention clock, and often no key to select on.\n\nHer framing, which I am adopting verbatim into `../pii-governance.md`: *\"I have not modelled it. I would rather say so than let its absence read as a decision that it doesn't exist.\"* It sits across 02 (never-log list) and 04 (log retention), and the cheapest control is upstream of this track entirely — if PII never enters a log there is nothing to delete from it.\n\n## Outcome\n\n`../migration-approach.md` is **final** and has gone to 04.\n\n---\n\n*Pattern footer (`../../CASTING.md` §5 ⟨02⟩). Objections raised: 6. Accepted: 6. Declined: 0. Cheapest pattern in the catalog; it overturned the reasoning behind the document's central requirement for one turn.*\n"

## AI tooling note

### Phase 4 — Jira (Warren's go: 2026-09-12, "start building out the backlog")

Team-lead is the single Jira writer (one authenticated session, one audit trail); leads author. Each lead writes `<track-dir>/backlog.md` — the in-repo, versioned backlog, which is itself a submission artifact — and team-lead transcribes it into project `LT` (cloudId `db488eae-d6cb-4100-92a9-e7a4519fc176`) and writes the issue keys back into the file.

`backlog.md` format, identical for every track:
- Header: track, lead, Epic title (team-lead creates one Epic per track; the key is written back here).
- One section per Story, in dependency order: **Title** (imperative, ≤ 80 chars); **Description** (what and why, 3–8 sentences, citing the deliverable and decision files by relative path); **Acceptance criteria** as a checklist — the first criterion is always the hand-off standard ("the receiving track can act on this without re-deriving the reasoning"), and every caller-visible guarantee names its enforcement site; **Depends on** (other tracks' story titles, which become Jira "blocks/is blocked by" links); **Model/effort** recommended for implementation (from `planning-approach.md`); **Type** Story or Task; **Labels** (`track-0N`, plus `security-graded` where 02's second-review rule applies, `no-code-yet` until Warren's code go).
- Stories arising from the consistency pass are added under whichever track owns the fix, with a `from-consistency-pass` label; a jointly owned story (e.g., the derived-data deletion gap across 02/04/05) is created once under the track that owns the enforcement site, with the other two linked as dependencies.

Order: 01 authors and is transcribed immediately (closed first, least touched by the pass); 05 has stories ready and follows; 02/03/04 author now and are transcribed the moment the consistency pass reports, so pass-driven revisions land once. Implementation still waits for Warren's explicit code go-ahead; "backlog strong enough to start implementation" is team-lead's call to Warren, defined as: every track's stories transcribed, every cross-track dependency linked, every pass finding either resolved or carried as a story, and 03's implementation stories each pointing at a stable contract.

Run as an adversarial pair; ruling and all objections in `decisions/search-query-shape.md`. Eight numbered objections raised, eight accepted, none declined. Four were live defects: `ORDER BY name` returning different page-1 rows per backend (PostgreSQL collation vs. SQLite `BINARY` byte order); `LOWER(name) LIKE` unable to use a GIN index declared on the bare column, so the accelerator accelerated nothing; caller input reaching a `LIKE` pattern unescaped; and `Country` normalized into the query but not into the table.\n\n**Acceptance criteria.**\n- [x] The receiving track can act on this without re-deriving the reasoning.\n- [x] Every objection closes with an explicit \"what would change my mind\";
